Against it: quiz-style Indian-state Shorts had a median of 63 views (35 Shorts, 31 channels) and 'quiz or guess' titles reach 1M in only 5.9% of cases. For it: Geo Facts' quiz Shorts do well globally (204K median), personalised and 'argue about it' hooks pull 7 to 12 times the median comment rate, and every round is a result that overturns a common belief. If the quiz fails, the content still stands as a ten-fact explainer.
- **Risks:** No Indian long-form precedent for a clue-quiz format, so expect high variance. Every round must have a clear gap (all are above 8% apart; avoid thin pairs). Numbers repeat other videos, so release it after the duels and tease them. Do not claim viewers 'get it wrong' without evidence. Template-factory risk: vary the round mix each time. Rounds that touch sensitive topics are excluded by design.
- **Effort:** high

### L064. 16 of 22 States and UTs Now Have Fewer Than 2.1 Children Per Woman

- **Original title:** India's Baby Gap: Bihar 2.8 Children Per Woman, Delhi 1.2
- **Status:** Not shortlisted (ranked lower). **Score** 4.2 (virality 4, appeal 5, distinct 4, fit 8, risk medium)
- **Series:** The Long Series
- **Template:** new: Long Series documentary on Long Kit v1 with a small-multiple 'curve per state' panel (22 mini line charts on a common axis), an 'India at replacement' reference line at 2.1 and a lag strip (how many years one state trails another).
- **Hook:** In 2004 the average woman in Bihar had 4.3 children. In 2023, 2.8. In Tamil Nadu: 1.3.
- **Outline:** Target 15 min. Ch1 The two numbers (1.5 min): children per woman 2023, Bihar 2.8, Delhi 1.2 (T006). Ch2 2004 vs 2023 (3 min): the map in both years; Uttar Pradesh 4.4 to 2.6, Bihar 4.3 to 2.8, Madhya Pradesh 3.7 to 2.4. Ch3 16 of 22 states below 2.1 (2.5 min): India 1.9; Tamil Nadu under 2.1 in every year since 2003. Ch4 Births (2 min): birth rate 2023, Bihar 25.8, Uttar Pradesh 23.6, Rajasthan 22.9, Tamil Nadu 12.0 (T002). Ch5 Babies who survive (2.5 min): infant mortality 2004 to 2023, Kerala 12 to 5, Tamil Nadu 41 to 12, Bihar 61 to 23, Uttar Pradesh 72 to 37 (T004). Ch6 Longer lives (1.5 min): life expectancy 2019-23, Kerala 75.1 to Chhattisgarh 64.6 (T007). Ch7 The lag chart (1 min): how many years Bihar trails Uttar Pradesh on the same curve. Ch8 What the next Census will count (0.5 min), then pause-and-guess on your state.
- **Who shares it and why:** A Bihar or UP viewer shares the 4.3-to-2.8 fall to counter a stereotype and aspirants forward it as notes, but nothing is surprising or contested enough to make anyone tag a friend.
- **Viral mechanism (writer's claim):** A surprise that overturns a belief ('it is not only the South'), plus 'your state' pride. A Bihar or UP viewer shares it to counter a stereotype, a Kerala or Tamil Nadu viewer shares the survival curve, and teachers and civil-services aspirants share it for the numbers. Expected comments: 'so will India's population shrink?', 'what about migration', 'where is Census 2027'. Share trigger: the 22-curve panel with the 2.1 line, and the 'Bihar 2004 4.3 to 2023 2.8' card.
- **Why it could flop:** Population by state is the saturated GK lane (84% of such Shorts under 1,000 views), and Census explainers have a median of only 29K. 'The South is low, Bihar is high' is what most viewers already believe, so the 'it is not only the South' surprise is mild. The topic is communal and delimitation-coded, so the comments will turn to religion and seats whatever the narration says. Fifteen minutes of births, infant deaths and life expectancy is a development tour with no single tension. Only 22 states and UTs report. Delhi 1.2 against Tamil Nadu and West Bengal 1.3 is a 0.1 gap at one decimal, so no 'lowest' claim is safe.
- **Key numbers (as proposed):** Children per woman (T006, 2023): Bihar 2.8, Uttar Pradesh 2.6, Madhya Pradesh 2.4, Rajasthan 2.3, Chhattisgarh 2.2, Jharkhand 2.1, Delhi 1.2, Tamil Nadu 1.3, West Bengal 1.3, Kerala 1.5, India 1.9; 16 of the 22 states/UTs are below 2.1. 2004 values: Bihar 4.3, Uttar Pradesh 4.4, Tamil Nadu 1.8, Kerala 1.7. Birth rate 2023 (T002): Bihar 25.8, Uttar Pradesh 23.6, Rajasthan 22.9, Tamil Nadu 12.0. Infant mortality (T004): Kerala 12 (2004) to 5 (2023); Tamil Nadu 41 to 12; Bihar 61 to 23; Uttar Pradesh 72 to 37; Madhya Pradesh 79 to 37. Life expectancy 2019-23 (T007): Kerala 75.1, Chhattisgarh 64.6.
- **Data editor:** not checked in this run; re-check every number before a script is written.
- **Source:** RBI Handbook T002 (birth rate), T003 (death rate), T004 (infant mortality), T006 (total fertility rate, Sample Registration System), T007 (life expectancy); nfhs6_states.json indicator 18 as a cross-check. (in hand)
- **Evidence it could work:** Launch slot 9. Population and census queries are the third-largest data theme in YouTube search (392 suggestions), and 'Why India and China Are So Populated' (3.4M) and 'This Indian State Has a Country's Population' (1.6M) are proven Shorts (research_shorts_india.md). Census 2027 gives a timeliness hook, though Census explainers had a median of only 29K, so timeliness helps but is not the engine. The surprise is that the fall happened everywhere, not just in the South.
- **Risks:** Only 22 states/UTs report fertility in T006, so smaller states are missing and the title must not say 'every state'. Below 2.1 does not mean population is about to fall (age structure and momentum), so make no forecast and avoid 'shrinking' wording. Keep strictly away from religion, caste or community splits and from delimitation seat talk. Bihar (2.8) and Uttar Pradesh (2.6) are close, so do not claim 'highest' without that pair on screen. Life-expectancy rows are three-year pools. SRS and NFHS values differ in method; label which is which.
- **Effort:** medium

### LG09. Women Who've Used the Internet: 33% to 64% (NFHS-5 to NFHS-6)

- **Original title:** NFHS-6 Report Card: Women Who've Used the Internet Nearly Doubled
- **Status:** Not shortlisted (ranked lower). **Score** 3.9 (virality 4, appeal 5, distinct 5, fit 6, risk medium)
- **Series:** Report Card
- **Template:** new: 16:9 'report card' on the Vox engine (an India NFHS-5 to NFHS-6 arrow per indicator, urban/rural bars, state-mover map, closing 'your state' quiz)
- **Hook:** In four years the share of Indian women who have used the internet went from 33% to 64%. Here is what else changed inside Indian homes.
- **Outline:** Target 13 min. 1 The phone and the internet (2.5 min): women who have used the internet 33.3% to 64.3%, men 51.2% to 80.5% (gap 17.9 to 16.2 points); women with their own mobile 53.9% to 63.6%; urban women online 77.3% vs rural 58.6%; biggest state jumps Andhra Pradesh 21.0% to 63.6% and Uttar Pradesh 30.6% to 70.0%. 2 Money and ownership (2): women with their own bank account 78.6% to 89.0%; women paid in cash for work 25.4% to 30.8%; homes where a woman owns house or land 14.0% to 18.8%; homes with health insurance 41.0% to 60.2%. 3 Birth (2): C-section births 21.5% to 27.2%; share of private-hospital births that were C-sections 47.4% to 54.1% (a fact, no blame). 4 Children (2): stunted children 35.5% to 29.3%; fully vaccinated 76.6% to 82.6%; babies 6-23 months with an adequate diet 11.0% to 15.3%. 5 Weight and sugar (2): overweight or obese women 24.0% to 30.7%, men 22.9% to 27.3%; high blood sugar men 15.6% to 20.9%, women 13.5% to 17.8%. 6 What went the other way (1): exclusive breastfeeding under 6 months 63.7% to 55.8%. 7 Urban vs rural (1): which gaps narrowed. 8 Your state's report card (1.5): three quick checks, unreliable cells ('*', '(x)') shown blank.
- **Who shares it and why:** Parents, women's groups and NRIs forward the 33% to 64% bar as proof of change in homes, and others dispute it ('not true in my village'), which adds comments but not much reach.
- **Viral mechanism (writer's claim):** 'Your home' personalisation plus a then-vs-now surprise (a number that nearly doubled). Shared by parents, students and NRIs with families in villages, and by women's-group and education-circle audiences. Likely comments: 'not true in my village', 'phones but no jobs', 'my state?', and corrections from people who know the survey. Share trigger: the 33.3% bar growing to 64.3% in one move with 'in four years' on screen.
- **Why it could flop:** Health and education Shorts under-index (lift 0.73), and society and health long-forms sit below the top-decile base (8.4% vs 9.7%); the 'stunting india' search seed returned nothing, so there is no proven search demand. Eighteen indicators over six chapters is a digest without an argument, and 13 minutes of arrows is closer to a report read-out than a story. The NFHS-6 file was built from text layers, OCR and hand checks, comparability with NFHS-5 is unchecked per indicator, and one wrong state jump would undercut the primary-source trust that is the channel's only edge.
- **Key numbers (as proposed):** India, NFHS-5 to NFHS-6: women used internet 33.3% to 64.3% (urban 77.3%, rural 58.6%); men 51.2% to 80.5%; women with own mobile 53.9% to 63.6%; women with own bank account 78.6% to 89.0%; women paid in cash 25.4% to 30.8%; woman owns house/land 14.0% to 18.8%; health insurance 41.0% to 60.2%; C-section births 21.5% to 27.2%; private-hospital C-sections 47.4% to 54.1%; stunted 35.5% to 29.3%; fully vaccinated 76.6% to 82.6%; adequate diet 11.0% to 15.3%; overweight women 24.0% to 30.7%, men 22.9% to 27.3%; high sugar men 15.6% to 20.9%, women 13.5% to 17.8%; exclusive breastfeeding 63.7% to 55.8%. State movers (women online): Andhra Pradesh 21.0% to 63.6%, Uttar Pradesh 30.6% to 70.0%; Goa 94.0% and Tripura 48.8% in NFHS-6.
- **Data editor:** not checked in this run; re-check every number before a script is written.
- **Source:** public/datayt/nfhs6/nfhs6_states.json (IIPS NFHS-6 India and State/UT Fact Sheets 2026; indicators 7, 10, 11, 14, 15, 38, 39, 44, 62, 68, 69, 76, 77, 80, 83, 91, 92, 93; index in scripts/datayt/nfhs6_meta.py). (in hand)
- **Evidence it could work:** Gap 6 (NFHS report card). NFHS-6 (101 indicators x 36 states, with NFHS-5 and urban/rural built in) is the only dataset STATE.md marks 'verified' and appears in at most S021, L052 and L064; batch-001 holds 100 NFHS Shorts but no long-form. Measured: then-vs-now Shorts reach 1M+ in 18.5% of cases vs 12.4%; health and education Shorts under-index (lift 0.73), so the report card opens on phones, bank accounts and insurance, the everyday items, and lives as a long-form where watch hours accrue; the 12-20 minute narrated place video is the repeatable breakout zone (45-48% at 3x+).
- **Risks:** NFHS-5 and NFHS-6 comparability (timing, definitions) must be checked per indicator in the IIPS fact sheet; the NFHS-6 file was built from text layers, OCR and hand checks, and cells marked '*' or '(x)' are unreliable. Some state jumps look suspicious (Jammu and Kashmir health insurance 13.8% to 96.3%, Ladakh 17.1% to 96.0%) and probably reflect scheme coverage definitions, so verify before featuring. Skip the spousal-violence and child-marriage indicators or word them neutrally. No cause claims (phones rising does not mean jobs rising). Health content under-indexes on Shorts, so do not cut one Short per chapter.
- **Effort:** medium

### L035. India's Richest States 2024-25: Telangana Is 2nd, or Is It 5th?

- **Original title:** Can You Sort India's States by Income? Official 2024-25 Data
- **Status:** Not shortlisted (ranked lower). **Score** 3.6 (virality 4, appeal 5, distinct 4, fit 5, risk medium)
- **Series:** Sort the States
- **Template:** new: 16:9 Sort-the-cards engine (five shuffled state cards, countdown, ranked reveal, current-price vs constant-price toggle)
- **Hook:** Five states. One number each. Put them in order before the clock hits zero.
- **Outline:** Target 12 minutes. 1) Rules and source (1 min): income per person, 2024-25, MoSPI via RBI; 25 states have data and the gap (Gujarat, Goa, Sikkim) is shown on screen. 2) Round 1, the South (1.5 min): Andhra Pradesh Rs 2,66,240, Kerala Rs 3,08,338, Tamil Nadu Rs 3,61,619, Karnataka Rs 3,80,906, Telangana Rs 3,87,623, with Telangana vs Karnataka stamped 'too close to call'. 3) Round 2, big by size, small per person (2 min): sort Maharashtra, Tamil Nadu, Uttar Pradesh, Karnataka and West Bengal by GSDP (T021), then by income per person. 4) Round 3, the North and the hills (1.5 min): Delhi Rs 4,93,024, Haryana Rs 3,53,182, Uttarakhand Rs 2,74,064, Himachal Pradesh Rs 2,56,137, Punjab Rs 2,21,197. 5) Round 4, the other end (1.5 min): Bihar Rs 69,321 up to Assam Rs 1,59,185. 6) The price twist (2 min): at constant prices (T020) Telangana drops from second to fifth (Rs 1,92,128) and Karnataka and Tamil Nadu move up. 7) The 7.1x gap between Delhi and Bihar and what the measure does and does not mean (1 min). 8) Your state: the full ranked list on screen (1 min). 9) Preview of the next Handbook edition and the vote for the next measure.
- **Who shares it and why:** Friends and classmates send 'sort these five, no cheating' with scores, and the Telangana price-toggle reorder is the one moment worth forwarding.
- **Viral mechanism (writer's claim):** A challenge that travels in group chats and classrooms: 'sort these five, no cheating', with scores sent back. Friends from the featured states argue ('where is Gujarat?' is the predictable comment, which the video answers by showing the data gap). The share trigger is the price toggle that moves Telangana from second to fifth, a surprise that overturns a belief while giving every viewer a reason to open the full list for their own state.
- **Why it could flop:** Ranking charts are the highest-variance format (40% at 3x+ but a 1.4x median), 'richest states' is crowded, and repeats decay (62K to 2.3K), so this must be a once-only definitive version, yet it is the video that most needs the data it lacks: Gujarat, Goa and Sikkim are missing from the 2024-25 column, so the 'richest' list leaves out states people most expect, and 'where is Gujarat?' is the top comment on a video that cannot fix it. Someone searching 'richest states of India' wants a ranked list, not a sorting game, so the game reduces search value without adding a share trigger. Five-card sorting for 12 minutes is repetitive and high effort. The Telangana reorder is the real hook, but current versus constant prices is jargon, and a base-year deflator effect is not an economic verdict. Telangana vs Karnataka is a 1.8% gap, so no ordering claim is safe.
- **Key numbers (as proposed):** T019 2024-25 (Rs): Delhi 493,024; Telangana 387,623; Karnataka 380,906; Tamil Nadu 361,619; Haryana 353,182; Maharashtra 309,340; Kerala 308,338; Uttar Pradesh 108,572; Bihar 69,321 (7.1x gap). T020 2024-25 constant prices (Rs): Delhi 283,093; Karnataka 204,605; Tamil Nadu 197,747; Haryana 194,285; Telangana 192,128. T021 2024-25 (Rs lakh): Maharashtra 453,151,842; Tamil Nadu 311,859,030; Uttar Pradesh 297,822,351; Karnataka 288,390,323.
- **Data editor:** not checked in this run; re-check every number before a script is written.
- **Source:** RBI Handbook of Statistics on Indian States T019 (per-capita NSDP, current prices), T020 (constant prices), T021 (GSDP); source: National Statistical Office, MoSPI. Next edition: the Handbook is usually published in December (not verified). (in hand)
- **Evidence it could work:** 'Richest states of India' is proven demand: Globe's 4.7-minute chart got 3.0M (118x its median), Data Vids 3.27M, Mohak Mangal 2.8M. But repeats decay (World In Details' eight near-identical versions went 62K to 2.3K), and ranking charts are a lottery (40% at 3x or more but median only 1.4x, 4-8 minute videos have a lower median than longer ones on 18 of 19 channels). So make one definitive official-source version, as a game, and refresh it annually. The existing winners use non-official numbers, which is Data Kadai's gap to claim.
- **Risks:** Gujarat, Goa, Sikkim and several small states are absent from the 2024-25 column: pull their own state statistical releases (mapped, not collected) or print the gap and avoid 'richest state' claims beyond the table. Telangana vs Karnataka differ by 1.8% at current prices, so no #2 claim. Per-capita NSDP is not household income. The Handbook date is not verified: if no new edition appears by mid-December, ship on 2024-25 and refresh later. Releasing many variants would repeat the decay seen on World In Details.
- **Effort:** high

### L034. North vs South India: Who Wins 10 Official Rounds?

- **Original title:** North vs South India: 10 Rounds, You Pick the Winner
- **Status:** Not shortlisted: high risk. **Score** 3.5 (virality 7, appeal 7, distinct 6, fit 5, risk high)
- **Template:** new: 16:9 Guess-the-winner engine (two-team scoreboard, split India map, 3-2-1 pause per round, 'too close to call' stamp; reusable for idea 14)
- **Hook:** Ten official numbers. South or North? Pick your winner before each reveal.
- **Outline:** Target 15 minutes. 1) The rules (1.5 min): teams are the Southern Zonal Council states vs the Northern and Central Zonal Council states (print the lists; verify membership); a round is judged on the group median and any gap under about 5% is 'too close to call'. 2) Rounds 1-2, money (2.5 min): income per person T019 (Telangana Rs 3,87,623, Karnataka Rs 3,80,906, Tamil Nadu Rs 3,61,619 vs Uttar Pradesh Rs 1,08,572, Madhya Pradesh Rs 1,52,615) plus the constant-price check T020. 3) Rounds 3-4, babies and lifespans (2 min): infant mortality T004 (Kerala 5, Tamil Nadu 12 vs Uttar Pradesh, Madhya Pradesh and Chhattisgarh 37) and life expectancy T007 (a near tie). 4) Rounds 5-6, work (2 min): rural construction wage T112 and women in the labour force 2025, PLFS (Tamil Nadu 47.3, Delhi 13.1, Himachal Pradesh 64.7, the North's best). 5) Rounds 7-8, phones and signal (2 min): 5G villages (Delhi 100%, Haryana 96.8%, Punjab 93.1% vs Telangana 19.1%, Andhra Pradesh 49.2%) and villages with no signal. 6) Round 9, prices (1.5 min): CPI inflation December 2025 (Kerala 9.49% vs India 1.33%). 7) Round 10, power (1 min): per-person availability T138. 8) Scoreboard and 'your state' card on a 36-state map (1.5 min). 9) What changes if Bihar and the East are added; vote for the next team split.
- **Who shares it and why:** Families and WhatsApp groups forward it as 'we won 6-3, come at me', with northern and southern relatives arguing over the rounds.
- **Viral mechanism (writer's claim):** Regional identity plus a game. Families and WhatsApp groups send it as 'we won 6-3, come at me'; comments: 'where is Bihar?', 'use population weights', 'my state is the outlier', 'Telangana wage data missing'. The share trigger is the scoreboard after each round and the honest 'too close to call' stamp, which makes the verdict feel earned rather than partisan. A first pass over the files gives the South the income, infant-mortality, wage and women-at-work rounds and the North and Central group the no-signal and inflation rounds, with several rounds too close to call, so neither side sweeps.
- **Why it could flop:** The measured hits (63% at 3x+) were provocative or one-sided titles, some of which our neutral-wording rule bars, so the careful version loses part of the engine. The result is decided by who is on each team (Southern Zonal Council against Northern plus Central), and group medians hide huge variance; the South probably wins most rounds on income, infant deaths and life expectancy, so either the game is lopsided or the 'too close to call' stamp looks engineered. A 3-2-1 pause ten times in 15 minutes is a gimmick that risks retention, and guess-the-winner has no measured long-form precedent. The lane is also crowded (19 videos on 8 channels, plus a 12.9M Dhruv Rathee video), and the small-channel hits were presenter-led. The scoreboard format itself is a 'who is better' frame, which the neutral-wording rule discourages. Engine and research effort are high.
- **Key numbers (as proposed):** T019 2024-25 (Rs): Telangana 387,623; Karnataka 380,906; Tamil Nadu 361,619; Madhya Pradesh 152,615; Uttar Pradesh 108,572. T004 2023: Kerala 5; Tamil Nadu 12; Uttar Pradesh 37; Madhya Pradesh 37; Chhattisgarh 37. PLFS 2025 women's labour force participation: Tamil Nadu 47.3; Himachal Pradesh 64.7; Delhi 13.1. 5G villages: Delhi 100%; Haryana 96.8%; Punjab 93.1%; Telangana 19.1%; Andhra Pradesh 49.2%. CPI Dec 2025: Kerala 9.49%; India 1.33%.
- **Data editor:** not checked in this run; re-check every number before a script is written.
- **Source:** RBI Handbook T019, T020, T004, T007, T112, T138; MoSPI PLFS (public/datayt/sources/mospi/plfs_annual.json, 2025 calendar year); MoSPI CPI (public/datayt/sources/mospi/cpi_general.json, Dec 2025); DoT 5G coverage (Lok Sabha Starred Q 470). Zonal council membership: Ministry of Home Affairs, to be verified (mapped in SOURCE_MAP as a government source, not collected). (in hand)
- **Evidence it could work:** North-vs-South is the strongest single long-form bucket measured: 19 videos on 8 channels, 63% at 3x or more of channel median, 37% at 10x, median 4.4x, with 380K-1.1M views on channels of only 5K-28K subscribers. Verdict frames ('who is winning') hit 3x in 43.5% of place videos, and 12-30 minute place videos are the repeatable zone (45%-48% at 3x). Angel One's head-to-heads got 61x and 136x their channel median. The quiz mechanic gives a faceless video the same tension that presenters supplied in the hits (all North-vs-South winners were presenter-led, a gap to cover with the pause-and-pick device).
- **Risks:** The team definition decides the result; print it and show what changes with Bihar and the East. Group medians hide huge variance (Delhi against Uttar Pradesh). Telangana is missing from the wage table and several northern states are missing from phone tables, so choose rounds with full coverage. Do not call a round 'better' or 'worse', and never use a high credit-deposit ratio as a win. Part of the topic's lift may come from the 2026 delimitation news (an assumption). Hard rules: no party, election, caste or religion rounds, no mockery of any state, and 'South is 100x better' framings are off-limits. English-only narration is unproven for Indian reach.
- **Effort:** high

### L048. Delhi's Income Per Person Is 7.1x Bihar's, Down From 8.5x in 2011

- **Original title:** India's Richest and Poorest States, 2011 to 2025: Is the Gap Closing?
- **Status:** Not shortlisted (ranked lower). **Score** 3.4 (virality 4, appeal 5, distinct 4, fit 4, risk medium)
- **Series:** State of the States
- **Template:** new: DK Every-Year Race (16:9): animated rank ladder (bump chart) and India map with a big year counter, 2011-12 to 2024-25, pausing at rank changes; official boundary map only
- **Hook:** Spoken: 'In 2011-12, Delhi's income per person was 8.5 times Bihar's. Today it is 7.1 times. But look at Bihar and Karnataka.' On screen: the year counter at 2011-12 and every state a dot.
- **Outline:** Target 18 min. 1. Cold open: the gap, 8.5x to 7.1x (1 min). 2. The 2024-25 table (2.5): Delhi 493,024; Telangana 387,623; Karnataka 380,906; Bihar 69,321 (7.1x); Telangana and Karnataka are 1.8% apart, so they are level. 3. Who is missing (1.5): only 25 of 34 states and UTs have 2024-25 values; Gujarat ends 2022-23, Goa and Sikkim 2023-24 (both above Delhi that year: 585,953 and 587,743 vs 459,408). 4. Every year, 2011-12 to 2024-25 (4): the animated race. 5. Climbers and sliders (3): current prices Karnataka 9th to 3rd, Telangana 8th to 2nd, Uttarakhand 4th to 9th, Puducherry 2nd to 8th; at constant prices Telangana is 5th, so the order depends on the price basis. 6. Is the gap closing? (3): Delhi to Bihar 8.51x to 7.11x (current prices); constant prices peaked at 9.47x in 2017-18 and is 7.79x now; Bihar as a share of Delhi 11.8% to 14.1%, but as a share of Karnataka 24.1% to 18.2%. 7. Your state (2): rank ladder per state; comment your state's rank. 8. What per-person income hides (1).
- **Who shares it and why:** People send 'where does my state stand' to family and alumni groups and re-share it each December, and fans of climbers like Telangana and Karnataka post their jump, which is the only real trigger.
- **Viral mechanism (writer's claim):** Pride plus 'your state' personalisation plus a reference asset. Shared as 'where does my state stand' in family and alumni groups; saved and re-shared each December. Comments: 'where is Gujarat / Goa?', 'Delhi is not a state', 'my state climbed X places'. Share trigger frame: the ladder where the lines cross at Telangana 8th to 2nd and Uttarakhand 4th to 9th.
- **Why it could flop:** It is the 18-minute, highest-effort build, aimed at the most crowded query in the research ('richest states'), where repeat videos decayed from 62K to 2K and the big hits were 4-5 minute charts, not 18-minute races. Bar-chart races are now low-odds. The clean headline is spoiled by caveats: only 25 of 34 states have 2024-25 data, Gujarat ends at 2022-23, Goa and Sikkim in 2023-24 sit above Delhi (585,953 and 587,743 vs 459,408), Delhi is not a state, and the order flips with the price basis (Telangana 2nd at current prices, 5th at constant). Telangana and Karnataka are 1.8% apart, so no rank claim between them. The 8.5x to 7.1x gap shift is modest, not startling.
- **Key numbers (as proposed):** Per-person NSDP, current prices (T019): 2024-25 Delhi 493,024; Telangana 387,623; Karnataka 380,906; Bihar 69,321 (7.1x). Delhi to Bihar ratio: 2011-12 8.51x, 2024-25 7.11x (25 states with 2024-25 data, my calc). Constant prices (T020): 2017-18 9.47x, 2024-25 7.79x. Top three at constant prices: Delhi, Karnataka, Tamil Nadu. Rank moves among those 25 states (current prices): Karnataka 9 to 3, Telangana 8 to 2, Uttarakhand 4 to 9, Puducherry 2 to 8. Bihar as share of Delhi 11.8% to 14.1%; of Karnataka 24.1% to 18.2% (current prices). Goa 585,953 and Sikkim 587,743 in 2023-24 vs Delhi 459,408.
- **Data editor:** not checked in this run; re-check every number before a script is written.
- **Source:** public/datayt/sources/rbi_handbook/ T019 (current prices) and T020 (constant prices), 14 years 2011-12 to 2024-25. Refresh after the next RBI Handbook (usually December). (in hand)
- **Evidence it could work:** 'Richest states of India' is proven demand: Data Vids 3.27M and Globe 3.0M (118x its median) on 4-5 minute charts, Mohak Mangal 2.8M on a 33-minute explainer. Repeats decay (62K down to 2K), so the research says publish one definitive official-source version and update it yearly. 'Every Year' maps on broad subjects are the best code-only global format (Ollie Bye's broad 'Every Year' videos: median 587K, 49% at 3x+). The twist (the gap with Delhi narrows, the gap with Karnataka widens) is new.
- **Risks:** Any 'richest' claim must be year-fair because several states stop before 2024-25; say so on screen. Delhi, Puducherry and Chandigarh are city-like economies. Current and constant prices give different orders (Telangana 2nd vs 5th). 2024-25 values may be provisional. Andhra Pradesh and Telangana series are recast after the 2014 split (check the RBI footnotes). The richest-states lane is crowded, so this must be the one definitive video, not a series of variants. Per-person income hides inequality inside states.
- **Effort:** high

### L071. Where Do Indians Actually Live? India's Population Map Explained

- **Status:** Not shortlisted (ranked lower). **Score** 3.4 (virality 4, appeal 6, distinct 4, fit 3, risk medium)
- **Series:** Where India Lives (spin-offs: 'Nobody Lives In ...' for Ladakh, Arunachal, the Thar)
- **Template:** new: DK Density Explainer (16:9, 12-14 min; dot-density map inside the official outline, ranked density bars, 'fold the map' beat). Warped cartograms only as clearly labelled 'not to scale' graphics, never as 'the map of India'.
- **Hook:** Draw India again, but give every state the size of its people. Here is what comes out.
- **Outline:** Target 13 minutes. 1) The map you know vs the map of people (Census state populations). 2) Density by state, from Delhi down to the emptiest (Census population and area). 3) The smallest set of states, then districts, that hold half of all Indians (computed from Census; do not state the numbers until computed). 4) Why people cluster where they do: rivers, plains, coasts (official geography sources). 5) The emptier quarter: low-density states and the villages with no signal at all, 8,748 nationally and 1,176 of Arunachal Pradesh's 5,993 (DoT). 6) Cities: urban share and the metros (Census). 7) What the new Census count will update (verify phase dates before use). 8) Find your district: district density map.
- **Who shares it and why:** Geography fans and hometown groups send the people-sized map ('look how big UP is'), but a population cartogram is a familiar visual and nothing in it is argued over.
- **Viral mechanism (writer's claim):** A curiosity gap plus 'your district' personalisation. Shared by geography nerds and in district and hometown groups ('look where my district falls'). Comments: 'my district is the densest in the state', 'this is why X needs more seats' (steer away), 'where is the Census 2027 version'. The frame that triggers the share is the transition from the familiar outline to the people-map.
- **Why it could flop:** It is a near-duplicate of L067 (same 'half of India' reveal, same missing Census data), and L067 has the stronger 'your district' personalisation, so only one should be built. Population-by-state is the saturated lane (84% of such Shorts under 1,000 views), and a state-sized cartogram is a staple of India infographics. The Census 2011 state data is not collected, and the fallback (implied population from RBI T023 and T019) is a derived estimate, not a primary count, against the channel's own rule. A warped outline clashes with the official-boundary rule. The 8,748 no-signal villages chapter is bolted on from an unrelated dataset. State-level data also gives weaker personalisation than district-level.
- **Key numbers (as proposed):** Villages with no mobile signal (DoT, 28 Feb 2026): 8,748 of 644,131 nationally; Odisha 1,177 and Arunachal Pradesh 1,176 (19.6% of its 5,993 villages). 5G reaches 57.0% of villages nationally. All density and population numbers must come from Census once collected; none are asserted here.
- **Data editor:** not checked in this run; re-check every number before a script is written.
- **Source:** NOT YET COLLECTED: Census of India 2011 state and district population, area and density (SOURCE_MAP: Census of India, censusindia.gov.in). In hand: public/datayt/coverage/mobile_coverage_2026-02-28.json (DoT answer, Lok Sabha Starred Q 470, 25 Mar 2026). Fallback while Census is collected: implied state population from RBI T023 / T019. (source mapped, not collected)
- **Evidence it could work:** Density and 'nobody lives here' is the best-evidenced code-only template: Geography By Geoff's 'Why So Few / Nobody Lives In [region]' series has 68 videos, median 1.15M views and a worst video of 139K; across 11 channels, density/empty/'live in' titles (n=132) run 1.46x with 27% at 3x+; Real Life Lore's 'Why Most Indians Live Above This Line' reached 3.6M; Gaurav Thakur's 'Why AUSTRALIA is 95% EMPTY?' reached 14M. The global research found no 8-15 minute India version in its sample, which is a gap. Population/census is a 392-suggestion search theme.
- **Risks:** Census 2011 is 15 years old and district boundaries have since changed: label it as 2011 and the 2011 boundaries. A warped cartogram changes the outline, which clashes with the official-boundary rule: use dot-density inside the official outline and show any warp only as a labelled graphic. Keep out Census religion, caste and language tables. Dates for the new Census phases are unconfirmed in our notes: verify before saying anything. Ladakh and J&K must use the official boundary.
- **Effort:** high

### L073. Telangana vs Andhra, Jharkhand vs Bihar: New States vs Parents

- **Original title:** Telangana, Jharkhand, Chhattisgarh, Uttarakhand: Where Are They Now?
- **Status:** Not shortlisted: high risk. **Score** 2.9 (virality 5, appeal 6, distinct 6, fit 8, risk high)
- **Template:** new: DK Long Scoreboard (16:9, parent vs child pairs, four chapters) with the DK Long Map Explainer chapters around it.
- **Hook:** In 2011-12, Telangana's income per person was 1.32 times Andhra Pradesh's. In 2024-25 it is 1.36 times.
- **Outline:** Target 13 minutes. 1) Four births: which state each came from and under which Act (Bihar, Madhya Pradesh and Uttar Pradesh Reorganisation Acts 2000; Andhra Pradesh Reorganisation Act 2014; verify from India Code). 2) Telangana vs Andhra Pradesh: real income per person (T020), infant mortality, SDG score. 3) Jharkhand vs Bihar. 4) Chhattisgarh vs Madhya Pradesh, including life expectancy (T007). 5) Uttarakhand vs Uttar Pradesh. 6) What we cannot say: our series starts in 2011-12, after three of the four splits, so there is no before-and-after and no verdict on whether splitting 'worked'. 7) Scoreboard: the new state is ahead on income in all four; the gap narrowed in two, was flat in one, widened slightly in one. 8) What to watch next (new Census, next Handbook).
- **Who shares it and why:** Natives and diaspora of Telangana, Jharkhand, Chhattisgarh and Uttarakhand post it in regional groups to show where their state stands against its parent, and parent-state viewers send it back as a rebuttal.
- **Viral mechanism (writer's claim):** How a place came to be, plus a rivalry between a child and its parent. Shared inside each pair's regional groups and the diaspora (Hyderabad, Dehradun, Ranchi, Raipur audiences). Comments: 'splitting was right/wrong' (steer back to the numbers), 'but look at X', 'where is my state's pair'. Trigger frame: the parent-child bar pair with the ratio changing from 1.32x to 1.36x.
- **Why it could flop:** It withholds the thing the winning precedents sold. Angel One's Telangana vs Andhra (494K, 61x) and Karnataka vs Tamil Nadu (1.1M, 136x) were 'who is winning' verdicts, and this outline ends with 'no verdict'. The hook is a gap that barely moved (1.32x to 1.36x). The data starts in 2011-12, after three of the four splits, so 'where are they now' has no 'then'. The pairs also give no clean scoreboard: Jharkhand's infant mortality is worse than Bihar's (29 vs 23) and Chhattisgarh trails Madhya Pradesh on life expectancy. Four pairs in 13 minutes is about 3 minutes each, with no single rivalry to argue over. Three of the four audiences are Hindi-belt and none of those states is in our top typed-state list. The one proven pair is already owned by Angel One. The cited 'Where are they now?' 2.8M hit is a nostalgia video about people from 1967, not evidence for states. The comment section will become 'bifurcation was right/wrong'.
- **Key numbers (as proposed):** Real income per person 2011-12 to 2024-25 (T020, constant prices) and ratio of new to parent state: Telangana 91,121 vs Andhra Pradesh 69,000 (1.32x) to 1,92,128 vs 1,41,609 (1.36x). Jharkhand 41,254 vs Bihar 21,750 (1.90x) to 68,357 vs 36,342 (1.88x). Chhattisgarh 55,177 vs Madhya Pradesh 38,497 (1.43x) to 93,161 vs 70,434 (1.32x). Uttarakhand 1,00,314 vs Uttar Pradesh 32,648 (3.07x) to 1,58,819 vs 55,990 (2.84x). Infant mortality 2023: Uttarakhand 20 vs Uttar Pradesh 37; Jharkhand 29 vs Bihar 23; Telangana 18 vs Andhra Pradesh 19; Chhattisgarh 37 vs Madhya Pradesh 37. SDG score 2023: Uttarakhand 79 vs Uttar Pradesh 67; Jharkhand 62 vs Bihar 57. Life expectancy 2019-23: Chhattisgarh 64.6 vs Madhya Pradesh 67.6.
- **Data editor:** not checked in this run; re-check every number before a script is written.
- **Source:** RBI Handbook T020 (constant-price income per person), T019, T004, T007, T106 (public/datayt/sources/rbi_handbook/). Reorganisation Act dates: India Code (not in SOURCE_MAP; hand-verify). (in hand)
- **Evidence it could work:** Origin-and-rivalry hooks are measured strengths: Angel One's 'Telangana vs Andhra Pradesh: Who is Winning?' got 494K views (61x its median) on a 28K-sub channel; India in Pixels' 'Where are they now?' reached 2.8M (5.2x); formation and merger Shorts hit 1M+ in 26.7% of cases (n=60). It is also a newer, neutral angle than the heavily covered 'division of UP' political explainers.
- **Risks:** Very sensitive for Telangana/Andhra Pradesh and for Jharkhand and Chhattisgarh identity politics: present only 'where are they now', never 'was it worth it'. No causal claims. Pre-split figures for Telangana are back-cast, so say so. Thin leads (for example SDG 74 vs 74, infant mortality 37 vs 37 are ties). Verify the Act years from India Code. Do not mention tribal or communal breakdowns.
- **Effort:** medium

### L068. Is Your State's Inflation Above or Below India's?

- **Original title:** India This Month: Prices, Jobs and Output, State by State
- **Status:** Not shortlisted (ranked lower). **Score** 2.6 (virality 2, appeal 3, distinct 6, fit 6, risk medium)
- **Series:** India This Month
- **Template:** new: Monthly Wrap 16:9 (7 to 9 min). Three fixed segments (prices from the CPI release, jobs from the PLFS release, output or GST) on the Mapped logic with one 'your state against India' card and one 'what changed since last month' strip. monthly_wrap.py pulls the three MoSPI releases through the existing collect_sources.py client and refuses to publish if a table is not state-wise or its base year changed.
- **Hook:** Is your state's inflation above or below India's this month?
- **Outline:** Target 8 min. Ch1 Prices (3 min): state-wise CPI inflation, rural, urban and combined, highest and lowest states, your state against India (MoSPI, released the 12th). Ch2 Jobs (2.5 min): unemployment and labour force from the PLFS release (the 15th; quarterly bulletin 10 Nov 2026 and 10 Feb 2027; annual report for 2026 on 25 Mar 2027), checking first which tables are state-wise. Ch3 Output or tax (1.5 min): IIP on the 28th, or GST collections by state on the 1st (gst.gov.in). Ch4 One thing to watch next month, with the release date (1 min). SCHEDULE, first ten wraps: 1 Nov 2026 (CPI 12 Nov, PLFS quarterly 10 Nov); 2 Dec 2026 (CPI 12 Dec, GDP Q2 of 30 Nov recapped); 3 Jan 2027 (CPI 12 Jan, First Advance Estimates 7 Jan); 4 Feb 2027 (Budget 1 Feb, CPI 12 Feb, PLFS quarterly 10 Feb, GDP Q3 26 Feb); 5 Mar 2027 (CPI 12 Mar, PLFS annual 25 Mar); 6 to 10 Apr to Aug 2027 on the same cadence, dates to be confirmed from the next MoSPI calendar.
- **Who shares it and why:** A subscriber may forward their own state's card to a family group on release day when it beats India, but nobody tags a friend for a monthly bulletin.
- **Viral mechanism (writer's claim):** Timeliness plus 'your state' personalisation, with low virality expected. A subscriber forwards the clip of their own state to a family group on release day; the monthly habit builds subscribers and watch hours and cuts easily into Shorts. Expected comments: 'my state is above India again', 'what about food prices', 'base year changed'. Share trigger: the single 'your state against India' card.
- **Why it could flop:** The idea's own 'why' calls it a trust engine, not a breakout engine, and the evidence agrees. 'State wise inflation' returned no search suggestions, GST and air-quality hooks have medians under 6K, and CPI hits above 1M came from very large creators. A zero-subscriber channel cannot beat news channels on release day, and the 'your state against India' card has no stakes or surprise. A monthly cadence also looks like a template factory. Each month needs a new data pull, tables may not be state-wise, and the 2026 CPI base-year change breaks comparison with the 2025 series in hand. That is a heavy load on a 5 to 7 hour week for little reach. The title as written is a label, not a hook.
- **Key numbers (as proposed):** Unemployment rate 2025 (PLFS, calendar year): India 3.1%; urban 4.8%, rural 2.4%; Gujarat 0.9%, Madhya Pradesh 1.5%, Goa 8.3%, Andaman and Nicobar Islands 10.0%.
- **Data editor:** not checked in this run; re-check every number before a script is written.
- **Source:** MoSPI eSankhyiki / API: CPI (public/datayt/sources/mospi/cpi_general.json holds 12 months of 2025, base 2012), PLFS (plfs_annual.json), IIP; MoSPI Advance Release Calendar 2026-27; GST statistics (gst.gov.in, SOURCE_MAP). New months not yet pulled. (source mapped, not collected)
- **Evidence it could work:** A timeliness and trust engine, not a breakout engine. Budget explainers have a median of 75K and a max of 906K, Economic Survey a median of 56K, CPI and inflation a max of 3.4M but only for a very large creator, and air quality, GST and literacy hooks all have medians under 6K (digest calendar). The state-by-state 'your state against India' card is what a generic wrap lacks, and the 12th, 15th and 28th of each month give a reason to return.
- **Risks:** Low upside and a new data pull every month, so keep to 7 to 9 minutes and drop a segment if the table is not state-wise. MoSPI revised the CPI base year in 2026, so check the base before comparing with the 2025 series in hand. Release dates 'may change'. Four to eight minute videos have lower medians than longer ones in 18 of 19 channels, so do not judge the channel by these. No investment or market commentary on prices or GST (SEBI rule).
- **Effort:** medium

### L081. Himachal Budgets Rs 34.70 of Every Rs 100 for Interest and Pensions

- **Original title:** Union Budget 2027 in Charts: Where Your Rs 100 Goes
- **Status:** Not shortlisted (ranked lower). **Score** 2.6 (virality 3, appeal 5, distinct 3, fit 3, risk medium)
- **Series:** Budget Week (Economic Survey the day before, Budget day, then a state-budget follow-up)
- **Template:** Breakdown (existing 100-square grid, re-cut to 16:9) plus Ranking (existing), inside the new DK Long Map Explainer.
- **Hook:** Of every Rs 100 Himachal Pradesh budgets for day-to-day spending, Rs 34.70 goes to interest and pensions.
- **Outline:** Target 11 minutes, published within 24 hours of the Budget. 1) The Budget's own picture: where the rupee comes from and where it goes (Union Budget at a Glance, to collect after the speech). 2) Three charts from the Economic Survey (published the day before; contents to read). 3) The same rupee for the states: interest plus pension as a share of revenue spending, 2024-25 budget estimates (T166/T170/T171). 4) Four states side by side: Himachal Pradesh, Kerala, Punjab, Tamil Nadu vs Uttar Pradesh, Maharashtra, Delhi. 5) Who funds their own spending: own tax revenue vs revenue spending (T168). 6) What the Finance Commission and GST transfers add (Lok Sabha U2721 'Tax Devolution Policy' 17.03.2025 and U1575 'GST Contribution and Compensation released to States' 09.02.2026, to read). 7) Your state's Rs 100 card. 8) What changes in the next Handbook (2025-26 figures).
- **Who shares it and why:** State-group members post their state's 'Rs 100' card on Budget day, but that card covers state budgets, not the Union Budget everyone is discussing.
- **Viral mechanism (writer's claim):** Personalisation plus timeliness. A per-state Rs 100 card is shared in that state's groups on Budget day, and the 'interest and pensions' squares are the shock. Comments will bring up the old pension scheme and freebies (political): moderate hard. Trigger frame: the 100-square grid with the interest and pension squares shaded.
- **Why it could flop:** The title promises the Union Budget, but no Union Budget data is in hand and none may be pre-stated. The actual content is 2024-25 state budget estimates, so a viewer who arrives for the Budget gets a mismatch. Budget day is the most crowded day of the year (explainers: median 75K, max 906K) with anchors, CAs and big channels, and a zero-subscriber faceless channel cannot win on speed. It is a 24-hour turnaround on a high-effort build, and a missed or shifted date leaves a time-boxed video worth almost nothing. These are budget estimates, not actuals. Pension and freebie arguments drag the comments political. The idea itself calls this the weakest virality evidence in the set.
- **Key numbers (as proposed):** Interest plus pension as a share of revenue expenditure, 2024-25 BE (computed from T166/T170/T171): Himachal Pradesh 34.7%; Kerala 34.4%; Punjab 34.4%; Tamil Nadu 30.1%; Uttar Pradesh 26.3%; Maharashtra 22.4%; Delhi 4.4%; Telangana 13.3%; Chhattisgarh 12.6%; Arunachal Pradesh 12.6%. Own tax revenue as a share of revenue spending: Maharashtra 66.0%; Karnataka 65.4%; Bihar 24.1%. Uttar Pradesh pension Rs 86,488 crore; Tamil Nadu interest Rs 62,456 crore (largest).
- **Data editor:** not checked in this run; re-check every number before a script is written.
- **Source:** In hand: RBI Handbook T166, T168, T170, T171, T173, T174 (2024-25 BE). NOT YET COLLECTED: Union Budget documents and Economic Survey (SOURCE_MAP: Union Budget, Economic Survey, indiabudget.gov.in); Finance Commission (SOURCE_MAP). To read: Lok Sabha answers ls18-s4-U2721, ls18-s7-U1575, ls18-s7-U1430, ls18-s4-U1725. (source mapped, not collected)
- **Evidence it could work:** Weakest virality evidence in this set, kept because the lens asks for it and because Budget week is a fixed, high-intent moment: Budget explainers have a median of 75K views and a max of 906K; Economic Survey videos a median of 56K. The plan is to lift it with the 'your state' personalisation that draws 7-12x the median comment rate, and with a visual (the 100-square rupee) the benchmark channels do not use. Tax, budget, debt and GST is a 124-suggestion search theme ('state wise gst collection').
- **Risks:** Time-boxed and must ship within a day of 1 February 2027 (date and Survey date to verify). Do not pre-state any Union Budget number. Budget estimates are not actuals. 'Interest plus pension' definitions must follow the RBI Handbook. No praise or blame of any government, no old-pension-scheme or freebie argument, no ranking states on a political basis. Lok Sabha tables must be read before any number is used.
- **Effort:** high

### LG11. Bengaluru vs Hyderabad vs Pune: Who Wins on Official Data?

- **Original title:** Pune, Hyderabad, Bengaluru: Which City Is Really Winning?
- **Status:** Not shortlisted: high risk. **Score** 2.6 (virality 6, appeal 7, distinct 6, fit 3, risk high)
- **Series:** City Duels
- **Template:** new: 16:9 three-city scorecard on the Vox engine (one indicator per chapter, a city's bar lights when it leads, closing grid, no composite winner)
- **Hook:** Bengaluru, Hyderabad, Pune: three cities, one official scorecard at a time. Count the wins.
- **Outline:** Target 14 min. 1 Why this question (1 min): Pune as 'the next Bengaluru' vs Hyderabad's pull. 2 The state behind each city (2): income per person Telangana 387,623, Karnataka 380,906, Maharashtra 309,340 rupees (RBI T019, 2024-25) and services output Maharashtra 25.27, Karnataka 17.91, Telangana 10.19 lakh crore rupees (T049, 2024-25, current prices). 3 People and growth (2): city populations and 2001-2011 growth (Census of India; the 2027 count is pending). 4 Homes (2.5): house price index by city (NHB RESIDEX, cross-checked with the RBI house price index for the cities it covers). 5 Air (1.5): CPCB annual air-quality days by city. 6 Clean and liveable (2): MoHUA Swachh Survekshan ranks and the latest Ease of Living Index edition. 7 The scoreboard (1.5): a grid of wins, no composite verdict. 8 What numbers cannot say (0.5): traffic, jobs, culture. 9 Pick the next duel (0.5): poll for Chennai, Kochi or Coimbatore. CUT-DOWN: one city-price Short from the same pull (home price index change by city).
- **Who shares it and why:** IT colleagues, alumni and NRIs from Bengaluru, Hyderabad and Pune forward the scoreboard grid to settle the argument, and Chennai and Kochi viewers demand inclusion.
- **Viral mechanism (writer's claim):** City identity and friendly rivalry with a strong but fair question ('which city is winning?'). Shared in IT-colleague, alumni and relocation groups across the three cities and by NRIs from each; Chennai and Kochi viewers demand inclusion. Likely comments: 'traffic!', 'rents', 'Chennai is missing', 'which is best for X'. Share trigger: the scoreboard grid where each city lights up on different rows, so every fan can screenshot their rows.
- **Why it could flop:** None of the city numbers exist in our files (house prices, air quality, cleanliness, Census city data), the releases are irregular, and city, urban agglomeration and state are different units, so a chapter can easily end up stale or non-comparable. 'No composite winner' removes the verdict, and 'Hyderabad vs Bengaluru' reached only 2.2x on Adarsh Gupta's channel (his Pune Silicon Valley video, 10x, is the better precedent). It is the highest-effort and highest-data-risk idea on the list for a cold first long-form, and the three-city frame will draw 'Chennai is missing' complaints.
- **Key numbers (as proposed):** State backdrop only: income per person (T019, 2024-25) Telangana 387,623, Karnataka 380,906, Maharashtra 309,340 rupees; services GSVA (T049, 2024-25) Maharashtra 252,668,917, Karnataka 179,102,019, Telangana 101,915,689 rupees lakh. No city-level numbers are collected yet.
- **Data editor:** not checked in this run; re-check every number before a script is written.
- **Source:** In hand (state backdrop): RBI Handbook T019, T049. Not collected (SOURCE_MAP): NHB RESIDEX, RBI Database on Indian Economy (house price index), CPCB, MoHUA (Swachh Survekshan, Ease of Living), Census of India. (source mapped, not collected)
- **Evidence it could work:** Gap 7 (city lane). Measured: city titles hit 3x+ in 27.3% of long videos vs 14.7% (n=205) and are 10.0% of outliers vs 5.4% of videos; Adarsh Gupta's 'Chandigarh Vs Rest of India' reached 853K (12x) and 'Pune will be the Silicon Valley of India?' 721K (10x); verdict and 'next' frames hit 3x+ 43.5% of the time; Infra Talks megaproject videos hold a steady 3x to 9x. Caveats built in: Hyderabad vs Bengaluru alone did only 2.2x, so the hook is a three-city scoreboard with a question, not a two-city duel; air-quality videos had medians under 6K, so air is one chapter; the biggest price hit (19M) is a political then-vs-now and is not used. Price Shorts hit 1M+ in 16% of cases (n=200), hence the price cut-down.
- **Risks:** Every city number needs collecting and a source check first; confirm each source is current (RESIDEX and Ease of Living releases are irregular) and drop any chapter whose data are stale. Cities, urban agglomerations and states are different units: label them and never present a state figure as a city figure. No composite winner. Keep rivalry light, no mockery of any city. Price Shorts on petrol or onion need browser-only sources (Consumer Affairs, PPAC) and are a separate build.
- **Effort:** high

### L027. India's Map, Every Year: When Each State and UT Took Shape

- **Original title:** India's Map, Every Year: How 36 States and UTs Were Born
- **Status:** Not shortlisted: high risk. **Score** 2.3 (virality 5, appeal 6, distinct 6, fit 5, risk high)
- **Template:** new: Every Year map. Present-day official-boundary outlines light up in the year each state or UT took its present form; big year counter; Act name on a caption bar; no historical borders drawn. A 45-second cut of the same animation runs as a Short.
- **Hook:** Watch India's 36 states and Union Territories appear, one year at a time.
- **Outline:** Target 11 min. 1) Cold open: the finished map and 'which state is the youngest?' 2) From the Constitution to the States Reorganisation Act, 1956 (Act text and effective date). 3) The 1960s: the split of Bombay State, Nagaland, Punjab and Haryana. 4) 1971 to 1975: Himachal Pradesh, the 1972 north-east reorganisation, Sikkim (caption-only, citing the Constitution amendment). 5) 1987: Goa, Mizoram, Arunachal Pradesh. 6) November 2000: the three new states. 7) 2014 to 2020: Telangana, Ladakh, the Dadra and Nagar Haveli and Daman and Diu merger. 8) Oldest and youngest, an 'age of your state' board, and the closing question. Each chapter's data is the Act and its date.
- **Who shares it and why:** UPSC teachers and aspirants and state-pride accounts share it as a revision aid, and viewers comment their state and its year.
- **Viral mechanism (writer's claim):** Mechanism: how a place came to be, plus rewatch and 'find your state'. Teachers, UPSC aspirants and state-pride accounts share it; every viewer comments their state and its year ('Goa 1987', 'Telangana is the baby'). Trigger frame: the year counter ticking while the map fills, and the 45-second cut that loops.
- **Why it could flop:** The 'Every Year' hits (History of India 9.4M) win on borders changing and empires rising, which is exactly what the official-boundary rule removes. Lighting up present-day outlines is a chronological colour-in with no conflict, no verdict and no surprise past 'Telangana is the youngest', so 11 minutes may not hold viewers. The Shorts side has real demand (Bharat Mapping's state-formation Shorts reached 864K and 1.7M) but also existing supply, and those are told as stories, not lists of Acts. No year is in our files; 36 dated entries need careful reading, and 'present form' is ambiguous (Goa was Indian from 1961 and a state from 1987; Puducherry, Delhi, Sikkim and the 2019-20 changes are all disputed by pedants). A dark map before an Act risks implying territory was not India. Effort is high on the least-ready data of the batch.
- **Key numbers (as proposed):** 36 states and UTs (28 states plus 8 UTs, as in the NFHS-6 file). No year is in our files: all years must be read from the Acts.
- **Data editor:** not checked in this run; re-check every number before a script is written.
- **Source:** The Acts and notifications on India Code and the Gazette of India (not in SOURCE_MAP; add them). The count of 36 comes from the NFHS-6 file (28 states plus 8 UTs). Outlines from public/datayt/india/geo.json (official boundary). (answer PDF still to read)
- **Evidence it could work:** 'Every Year' maps are the best-evidenced code-only long format: Ollie Bye's 54 'Every Year' titles have a median of 519K (2.5x its channel median), broad subjects 587K with 49% at 3x+, and 'History of India: Every Year' reached 9.4M (45x). That hit needs historical borders, which our official-boundary rule bars, so this version uses only present-day outlines. 'How a place came to be' Shorts have a median of 383K. Format caveat: EmperorTigerstar's newest 'Every Year' videos sit at 57K to 59K, so subject breadth is the lever, and 36 places is broad.
- **Risks:** Needs a dated Act for 36 entries: several evenings of careful reading. Predecessor states (Bombay State, Madras State, Hyderabad State) cannot be drawn without historical boundaries: decide before building whether to show a neutral grey 'predecessor' block or captions only. Sensitive events (Hyderabad 1948, Goa 1961, Sikkim 1975, Jammu and Kashmir 2019) get caption-only, Act-only treatment. Use the Survey of India official outline for Jammu and Kashmir and Ladakh. A cold-start channel needs repeat uploads to reach 4,000 watch hours; retention is unmeasured.
- **Effort:** high

### L050. Bihar's Women Labour Force Passed Delhi's: 4.1% to 30.5%

- **Original title:** Bihar's Women in the Labour Force: 4.1% to 30.5% in 6 Years
- **Status:** Not shortlisted: high risk. **Score** 2.3 (virality 4, appeal 6, distinct 5, fit 8, risk high)
- **Series:** Why Desk
- **Template:** new: DK Why Desk Long (shared) with an India map that fills by value for 2017-18 and 2023-24, a slope chart per state, and a separate 'different clock' panel for the calendar-year readings
- **Hook:** Spoken: 'In 2017-18, four in a hundred women in Bihar were in the labour force. By 2023-24, thirty. Delhi stayed under nineteen.' On screen: Bihar's line climbing past Delhi's.
- **Outline:** Target 14 min. 1. Cold open (1 min): Bihar 4.1 to 30.5, Delhi 14.3 to 18.5. 2. What the number counts (2): working or looking for work, including unpaid family work (PLFS usual status, age 15+); India 23.3 to 41.7. 3. The biggest climbers (2.5): Arunachal Pradesh 14.7 to 66.5, Nagaland 16.7 to 64.3, Assam 12.7 to 50.2, Tripura 12.5 to 47.1, Jharkhand 15.4 to 49.8. 4. Who did not move (2): Delhi, Haryana 14.3 to 24.2, Goa 30.9 to 29.3. 5. Rural vs urban (2): India rural 24.6 to 47.6, urban 20.4 to 28.0; Bihar rural 3.9 to 32.0, urban 6.4 to 16.7. 6. A different clock (2.5): calendar-year 2024 and 2025 readings (India 40.3 and 40.0; Bihar 27.1 and 24.7; Delhi 17.9 and 13.1); do not join the lines. 7. What the data cannot prove (1.5): pay, hours, type of work, reasons; Lok Sabha answers to read (rural women, SHE-LEAPS). 8. Your state (1).
- **Who shares it and why:** Bihar and Jharkhand viewers share the jump with pride, and gender-and-work readers share it as a surprise, but the 'is it real?' reply arrives immediately.
- **Viral mechanism (writer's claim):** Pride and surprise, plus personalisation. Shared by Bihar and Northeast viewers and by gender-and-work audiences, around Women's Day (a known fixture, not in our notes) and the PLFS annual release. Comments: 'is it paid work?', 'what happened in 2025?', 'rural self-employment'. Those are fair questions we already answer in chapters 2, 6 and 7. Share trigger frame: Bihar's line crossing Delhi's.
- **Why it could flop:** The 4.1% start looks like a measurement or definition artifact, and our own file shows the calendar-year 2024 and 2025 readings falling (Bihar 27.1 then 24.7, Delhi 17.9 then 13.1), so the headline may not survive a careful commenter. It is also the same PLFS table as L060 and L051, so it competes with them. Bihar place videos hit 3x+ only about 17% of the time ('Bihar vs Rest of India' got 0.3x), the audience skews Hindi, and one-state-plus-other-states pacing over 14 minutes is thin.
- **Key numbers (as proposed):** Female labour force participation, age 15+, usual status (PLFS): India 23.3 (2017-18) to 41.7 (2023-24). Bihar 4.1 to 30.5 (rural 3.9 to 32.0, urban 6.4 to 16.7). Arunachal Pradesh 14.7 to 66.5; Nagaland 16.7 to 64.3; Assam 12.7 to 50.2; Tripura 12.5 to 47.1; Jharkhand 15.4 to 49.8; Delhi 14.3 to 18.5; Goa 30.9 to 29.3; Haryana 14.3 to 24.2; Uttar Pradesh 13.5 to 34.5. Calendar year 2024 and 2025: India 40.3 and 40.0; Bihar 27.1 and 24.7; Delhi 17.9 and 13.1. India rural 24.6 to 47.6, urban 20.4 to 28.0.
- **Data editor:** not checked in this run; re-check every number before a script is written.
- **Source:** public/datayt/sources/mospi/plfs_annual.json (LFPR, WPR, UR by state, sex and sector, 2017-18 to 2025). Context answers to read before scripting: Lok Sabha U2734 (Socio-Economic Upliftment of Rural Women, Rural Development, 04.08.2026) and U2580 (Operationalisation of SHE-LEAPS, 04.08.2026). Next data: PLFS annual report for 2026 due 25 March 2027 and the quarterly bulletin 10 Nov 2026 (MoSPI calendar). (in hand)
- **Evidence it could work:** Research ranks 'your state / your district' personalisation as the hook with comment rates 7 to 12 times the median (0.09% to 0.15% vs 0.013%), and state-data paradoxes as a breakout type (4.6M-view Bihar Short). Jobs, unemployment and wages are the third-biggest search theme (302 suggestions). Bihar going from last place to above Delhi is a belief-overturning story that fits 'how did this state go from last to ...'.
- **Risks:** The file's reference period changes from July-June years to calendar years, and MoSPI may have changed the survey design in 2025; read MoSPI's comparability note before drawing any trend across the break, and show the calendar-year dip rather than hide it. The measure includes unpaid family work and says nothing about pay or quality. Small states and UTs (Lakshadweep, Goa) have noisy estimates. Never claim a cause (schemes, self-help groups) without reading the answers. Keep it away from religion, caste or party breakdowns.
- **Effort:** medium

### L065. Kerala, Punjab, Himachal: Over a Third Goes to Interest, Pensions

- **Original title:** In 3 States, Over ₹34 of Every ₹100 Goes to Interest and Pensions
- **Status:** Not shortlisted: high risk. **Score** 2.1 (virality 4, appeal 4, distinct 7, fit 8, risk high)
- **Series:** The Long Series
- **Template:** new: Long Series documentary on Long Kit v1 with a 'where the ₹100 goes' stacked bar per state (interest, pensions, the rest, with social-sector spending as a separate strip) and a 20-year line of interest share from 2004-05.
- **Hook:** Before a state builds a road or a school, how much of its revenue spending is already spoken for? In Kerala, Punjab and Himachal Pradesh, over a third.
- **Outline:** Target 15 min. Ch1 The ₹100 rule (2 min): revenue expenditure, interest payments and pensions in the RBI state finance tables, 2024-25 budget estimates (T166, T170, T171). Ch2 The three above a third (2.5 min): Himachal Pradesh 34.7%, Kerala 34.4%, Punjab 34.4%. Ch3 The big states side by side (3 min): Tamil Nadu 30.1%, Haryana 29.9%, Uttar Pradesh 26.3%, West Bengal 26.1%, Karnataka 24.6%, Maharashtra 22.4%. Ch4 The low end (1.5 min): Telangana 13.3%, Odisha 14.9%, Chhattisgarh 12.6%, with the Delhi pension quirk explained. Ch5 Twenty years of interest (2.5 min): interest payments over revenue spending, 2004-05 to 2024-25, for 31 states. Ch6 Contingent promises (1.5 min): outstanding guarantees, Telangana 2,20,607 crore and Andhra Pradesh 1,55,075 crore (T177). Ch7 What is left (1 min): social-sector spending share (T175). Ch8 Pause-and-guess: where is your state. Cut Shorts: 'the ₹100 split for your state' for each of 10 states.
- **Who shares it and why:** Exam aspirants and finance-curious viewers forward their own state's stacked bar, but a fiscal-policy stat that reads as a political fight rarely gets tagged to a friend.
- **Viral mechanism (writer's claim):** A strong but fair question ('where does the money go before the work starts?') that every state's residents can test on their own state. Students and aspirants share it for exam value, local journalists and policy readers quote the card. Expected comments: 'old pension scheme', 'Centre vs state', 'Kerala debt', 'my state is low, why?'. Share trigger: the stacked ₹100 bar for one state, repeated across ten states, ending on the three above 34.
- **Why it could flop:** Terms like 'revenue expenditure' and 'committed spending' lose a cold viewer in 30 seconds, and budget explainers have a 75K median. This is the most politically hot topic in the set (old pension scheme, Kerala debt), so every comment thread becomes a party fight that our rules forbid us to join. 'Of every Rs 100' reads as total spending, but the denominator is revenue expenditure only, and the figures are 2024-25 budget estimates, not actuals. The shares are our own computation from T166, T170 and T171, so definitions must be checked against RBI's. Kerala, Punjab and Himachal sit within 0.3 points (34.4, 34.4, 34.7), so no ranking among them. Pensions are an obligation to retirees, so 'already spoken for' can read as an attack.
- **Key numbers (as proposed):** Share of revenue expenditure going to interest plus pensions, 2024-25 BE (computed from T166, T170, T171): Himachal Pradesh 34.7%, Kerala 34.4%, Punjab 34.4%, Tamil Nadu 30.1% (17.9 + 12.2), Haryana 29.9%, Uttar Pradesh 26.3%, West Bengal 26.1%, Karnataka 24.6%, Maharashtra 22.4%, Odisha 14.9%, Telangana 13.3%, Chhattisgarh 12.6%. Interest payments (T170): Tamil Nadu 62,456 crore, Maharashtra 56,727, Uttar Pradesh 53,712. Pension (T171): Uttar Pradesh 86,488 crore, Maharashtra 59,817, Tamil Nadu 42,509. Guarantees (T177, 2024 RE): Telangana 2,20,607 crore, Andhra Pradesh 1,55,075.
- **Data editor:** not checked in this run; re-check every number before a script is written.
- **Source:** RBI Handbook of Statistics on Indian States T164 (gross fiscal deficit), T166 (revenue expenditure), T168 (own tax revenue), T170 (interest payments), T171 (pension), T175 (social sector expenditure), T177 (outstanding guarantees); 31 states with 2024-25 budget estimates. (in hand)
- **Evidence it could work:** Launch slot 10. 'Truth About Tamil Nadu's Debt' (2.8M, Map Motion) is the one state-economy Short with a real hit, tax, budget and debt are 124 YouTube suggestions, Budget explainers have a median of 75K and a max of 906K, and the Union Budget (1 Feb 2027) and state budgets (Feb-Mar) fall in the next six months (research_shorts_india.md, digest calendar). It is the most 'your state' topic in the set and every state gets a card.
- **Risks:** Highest political-heat topic in the set: state governments of every party appear, so show all 31 on equal terms, never mention parties or chief ministers, and keep adjectives out. These are budget estimates for 2024-25, not actuals; say so on screen. Delhi shows a pension of only 3 crore, a reporting quirk, so footnote it. Himachal, Kerala and Punjab are within 0.3 points, so say 'about a third, three states close together', not 'highest'. Capital expenditure and gross fiscal deficit series show jumps (Punjab capital expenditure +1,229%) that may be definition changes: do not use without checking. Education and data only: no state bond advice (SEBI rule).
- **Effort:** medium

### L080. Gujarat 0.9%, Goa 8.3%: India's Unemployment Rate by State

- **Original title:** India's Unemployment Rate Fell From 6.0% to 3.1%: State by State
- **Status:** Not shortlisted: high risk. **Score** 2 (virality 4, appeal 5, distinct 4, fit 8, risk high)
- **Series:** Jobs Report (after each PLFS release)
- **Template:** Change (existing, re-cut to 16:9) plus Mapped (existing), inside the new DK Long Map Explainer.
- **Hook:** India's official unemployment rate has about halved since 2017-18. Goa's is 8.3%. Gujarat's is 0.9%.
- **Outline:** Target 12 minutes. 1) The headline: unemployment 6.0% (2017-18) to 3.1% (2025); participation 49.8% to 59.3%; worker ratio 46.8% to 57.4%; note the two reporting bases. 2) What the rate counts and does not (definition, from the MoSPI note). 3) The map for 2025: Andaman and Nicobar Islands 10.0, Goa 8.3, Lakshadweep 6.9, Nagaland 6.7 ... Gujarat 0.9, Madhya Pradesh 1.5. 4) City vs village: urban 4.8 vs rural 2.4, and the exceptions (Goa rural 11.9 above urban 6.1; Arunachal Pradesh urban 15.6 vs rural 4.9). 5) Women vs men (national 3.1 vs 3.1; Kerala 6.4 vs 3.1). 6) Low unemployment and pay are different questions: rural construction wages (T112) for Gujarat, Madhya Pradesh and Kerala. 7) What the rate does not show: job quality (Lok Sabha S323, 'Trends in Real Wages and Job Quality', 16 Mar 2026, to read). 8) The next releases: PLFS quarterly (10 Nov 2026, 10 Feb 2027) and annual (25 Mar 2027).
- **Who shares it and why:** Jobseekers and exam-prep groups quote the Goa 8.3% vs Gujarat 0.9% split in jobs debates, mostly as ammunition rather than as a share.
- **Viral mechanism (writer's claim):** A strong but fair question: is 3.1% the right number to look at? Shared in jobseeker, exam-prep and college groups and quoted in debates about jobs. Comments will argue the definition ('one hour of work counts') and the state contrasts (Goa vs Gujarat). Trigger frame: the Goa 8.3% | Gujarat 0.9% split, then the pay panel.
- **Why it could flop:** It tells a job-anxious audience that unemployment has halved, which many will read as official-data apologetics. The comments will be 'one hour of work counts' and 'fake data', and our neutrality rule forbids taking a side in them. Gujarat 0.9% hands partisans a Gujarat-model fight. The headline trend (6.0% in 2017-18 to 3.1% in 2025) crosses a fiscal-year and a calendar-year basis, so the clean 'halved' claim is not clean. Unemployment and salary by state is a saturated lane (23 Shorts, 91% under 1,000 views), and 'state wise unemployment rate' returned nothing in autocomplete, so people do not search it this way. Small UTs are volatile, so the top of the map is noise. The 'is 3.1% the right number?' framing is the interesting part, but it is a seminar, not a share.
- **Key numbers (as proposed):** India unemployment rate (PLFS, 15+, usual status): 6.0% (2017-18), 3.2% (2023-24), 3.1% (2025); urban 7.7% to 4.8%, rural 5.3% to 2.4%. Participation 49.8% to 59.3%; worker ratio 46.8% to 57.4%. 2025 by state: Andaman and Nicobar Islands 10.0; Goa 8.3; Lakshadweep 6.9; Nagaland 6.7; Arunachal Pradesh 6.6; Delhi 5.9; Gujarat 0.9; Madhya Pradesh 1.5; Dadra and Nagar Haveli and Daman and Diu 1.6; Chhattisgarh 2.3; Karnataka 2.3. Goa rural 11.9 vs urban 6.1; Arunachal Pradesh urban 15.6 vs rural 4.9; Kerala female 6.4 vs male 3.1. Rural construction wage 2024-25 (Rs/day): Kerala 922.6; Gujarat 355.3; Madhya Pradesh 307.8.
- **Data editor:** not checked in this run; re-check every number before a script is written.
- **Source:** PLFS annual JSON public/datayt/sources/mospi/plfs_annual.json (UR, LFPR, WPR); RBI T112 (wages) and T008/T009 (older unemployment series). To read: Lok Sabha Starred Q 323 answered 16.03.2026 (Labour and Employment), and the MoSPI PLFS report (SOURCE_MAP: MoSPI reports). (in hand)
- **Evidence it could work:** Jobs and unemployment is a 302-suggestion search theme, and 'unemployment in india' is typed nationally (the state-wise phrasing returned nothing), so the state-by-state paradox is the differentiator. Alarm and affordability frames in this niche do land (Finshots TV 'Why Middle-Class Indians Will Never Afford Homes Again' 230K = 5.2x), but honest caveat: general jobs topics are not a proven outlier engine here, so the moderate claim is search plus argument. The PLFS release calendar (monthly on the 15th, quarterly 10 Nov and 10 Feb, annual 25 Mar) gives repeatable timeliness.
- **Risks:** Highly contested: no blame or praise for any government or party, no 'jobs scam' or 'fake data' framing. 2017-18 (fiscal year) and 2025 (calendar year) are different reporting bases, so label them. Small UTs and states are volatile (Chandigarh female 14.2 vs male 2.0). The definition of 'employed' must be read from MoSPI, not recalled. Do not claim low unemployment means good jobs.
- **Effort:** medium

### L078. Bihar 2.8 Children, Delhi 1.2: India's Two Futures

- **Original title:** India in 2047: What the Official Numbers Say (Not Predictions)
- **Status:** Not shortlisted: high risk. **Score** 1.5 (virality 4, appeal 6, distinct 7, fit 3, risk high)
- **Template:** new: DK Long Map Explainer (16:9, 14-16 min) with side-by-side population-pyramid beats.
- **Hook:** India's fertility rate is 1.9, below replacement. Bihar's is 2.8. Delhi's is 1.2. Same country, 2047.
- **Outline:** Target 15 minutes. 1) Today: fertility by state 2023 (T006): 16 of 22 states are already below the 2.1 replacement line. 2) Who is already older: share of people aged 60+ (NFHS-6): Kerala 20.7% vs India 12.9% vs Uttar Pradesh 10.3%. 3) India's national path to 2047: population, median age, share 60+ from the UN medium projection (to collect). 4) State projections: the official state-level projection stops at 2036, so we show that and say so; we do NOT stretch it to 2047. 5) What a high-income country would need: today's income per person by state vs the World Bank high-income threshold and the Viksit Bharat goal. 6) Jobs and pensions: what a bigger working-age group and a bigger 60+ group each need. 7) Your state in 2036 and what changes by 2047. 8) What would change these numbers: migration, fertility, Census 2027.
- **Who shares it and why:** Families forward it between generations ('Kerala is already old') and friends in different states tag each other, but only if the Bihar-vs-Kerala pyramids can actually be built.
- **Viral mechanism (writer's claim):** Future curiosity plus 'your state'. Shared in family groups (grandparents and grandchildren) and between friends in different states, with comments about migration, jobs and pensions. The frame that triggers the share is two pyramids side by side (Bihar vs Kerala) with the same national total.
- **Why it could flop:** The title promises 2047 and the data cannot deliver it. Official state projections stop at 2036 and the idea rightly refuses to stretch them. The UN national path and the state age-structure data behind the signature Bihar-vs-Kerala pyramids are not collected. What is in hand is 2023 fertility and NFHS-6 shares aged 60+, so the hook 'Bihar's is 2.8, Delhi's is 1.2, Same country, 2047' mixes 2023 numbers with a 2047 label. 'Projections, not predictions' has to be said on screen and deflates the curiosity gap. Fertility by state leads quickly into north-south delimitation and religion fights. The 5.3M precedent (India in Pixels) was a nostalgia video about a TV show's predictions, not a data forecast. Effort is high with the most uncollected sources of any idea.
- **Key numbers (as proposed):** Total fertility rate 2023 (T006): India 1.9; Bihar 2.8; Uttar Pradesh 2.6; Madhya Pradesh 2.4; Rajasthan 2.3; Chhattisgarh 2.2; Jharkhand 2.1; Tamil Nadu 1.3; West Bengal 1.3; Delhi 1.2 (6 of 22 states at or above 2.1, 16 below). Population aged 60+, NFHS-6: Kerala 20.7%; Goa 17.2%; Himachal Pradesh 16.4%; Tamil Nadu 16.3%; India 12.9%; Uttar Pradesh 10.3%; Meghalaya 6.8%. Income per person 2024-25 (T019): Delhi 4,93,024; Bihar 69,321.
- **Data editor:** not checked in this run; re-check every number before a script is written.
- **Source:** In hand: RBI T006 (fertility 2023), NFHS-6 public/datayt/nfhs6/nfhs6_states.json (indicator 3), RBI T019 (income per person). NOT YET COLLECTED: UN World Population Prospects (SOURCE_MAP), World Bank income classification (SOURCE_MAP: World Bank Open Data), IMF World Economic Outlook, state population projections to 2036 (MoHFW / National Commission on Population Technical Group report: publisher mohfw.gov.in is in SOURCE_MAP, the report itself is not individually mapped; to locate), IIPS LASI for ageing (SOURCE_MAP: IIPS), NITI Aayog for Viksit Bharat documents (SOURCE_MAP). (source mapped, not collected)
- **Evidence it could work:** Future and what-if framing inside the place cluster hit 3x+ in 45% of 31 videos (32% top-decile, median 2.8x), and India in Pixels' prediction-style video reached 5.3M (9.9x). 'India in 2047' is typed often (57 suggestions plus 110 year-2050+ mentions), but the visible results are AI-futurism, not data, so an official-numbers version stands apart. 'Your state in 2047' is a personalisation hook (comment rates 7-12x the median).
- **Risks:** Projections are not predictions: say so in the title and on screen, and do not extend state projections past the official end year. Fertility differences can be pushed into religion or north-south politics: keep religion out entirely and avoid delimitation framing. SRS fertility (T006) and NFHS-6 are different surveys: label each. Dates and wording of the Viksit Bharat goal must be read from the source, not remembered. Never state a 2047 number we have not collected.
- **Effort:** high
